The proof reduces variation to a quantitative estimate for finitely many disjoint annuli. For an integer $n\ge1$, define <a id="eq:count-definition"></a>$$\label{eq:count-definition}
 \mathcal S_n(F,G)
 =\sup\left\{\sum_{j=1}^m|B_{\varepsilon_j,R_j}(F,G)|:
 \begin{array}{l}
 0\le m\le n,\quad 0<\varepsilon_j<R_j,\quad
     \varepsilon_j,R_j\in\mathbb Q,\\
 (\varepsilon_j,R_j)\text{ pairwise disjoint}
 \end{array}\right\}.$$ Intervals may share endpoints, and the empty sum is zero. As in the variation, the entire choice is made separately at every output point.

<a id="stmt-thm-count"></a>
<div id="thm:count" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:count-bound"></a>
**Theorem 2** (Disjoint-annulus count estimate). *There is an absolute constant $C$ such that, for every integer $n\ge1$ and every complex $F,G\in L^3(\mathbb R^2)$, $$\label{eq:count-bound}
 \|\mathcal S_n(F,G)\|_{3/2}
 \le C\sqrt n\log(2+n)\|F\|_3\|G\|_3.$$*

</div>

Theorem <a href="#thm:variation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:variation">1</a> follows by arranging the increments of each partition in decreasing order and grouping their ranks dyadically. The resulting series has terms bounded by $C(1+j)2^{j(1/r-1/2)}\|F\|_3\|G\|_3$. The substance of the paper is therefore <a href="#eq:count-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:count-bound">[eq:count-bound]</a>.

For smooth annuli, we write the kernel as a scale integral of convolutions of three one-variable Gaussian derivative windows. Each convolution is an integral over window centers whose sum is zero; with the centers fixed, the three window factors depend separately on $a,b,c$. On a finite grid the resulting sums are cyclic matrix traces: the sampled pairwise inputs supply three matrices, and the window factors supply diagonal insertions. We localize the matrices with positive Gaussian weights. At each vertex we place the two incident weighted matrices side by side to form a row matrix $R$. The energy $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}((R^*R)^{3/2})$ has the same degree-three scaling as the trilinear form. A mixed trace inequality bounds the localized trace by quadratic costs from the Hessians of these energies. With the matrices fixed, heat identities control the accumulated costs by initial energies after integration over centers. Those integrated energies are bounded by sums of cubed $L^3$ norms.

There are two different costs in passing from one annulus to many. For smooth annuli, entries of the matrix formed from the dual input switch on and off at their chosen endpoints. We estimate the energy jumps entrywise, then rescale the dual input to obtain a $\sqrt n$ bound. For hard annuli, estimating each endpoint error separately would give a linear loss. We instead group endpoints in dyadic radius intervals. Disjointness gives uniform size and derivative bounds for each group even when it has many jumps.

The main additional analytic tool is the frequency-block estimate of Proposition <a href="#prop:frequency-block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:frequency-block">17</a>. It handles modulated Gaussian windows with coefficients depending jointly on scale, frequency, and output position. For uniformly bounded coefficients, a pointwise square-integral bound over scales and frequencies suffices, uniformly in the frequency size. To obtain that uniformity, we narrow one Gaussian variance and average its center shifts. This expresses each oscillatory insertion as an average of derivatives of narrower Gaussians. Auxiliary heat flows retaining only one input edge control the extra derivative terms. A two-dimensional Gaussian estimate then bounds the energy changes at the ends of the scale intervals without a loss from the narrow variance. This estimate and the rough-kernel family bound in Proposition <a href="#prop:rough-family" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:rough-family">21</a> are formulated separately from the annular application.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Organization

Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a> fixes truncations, coordinates, and line maximal estimates. Section <a href="#sec:matrix" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:matrix">3</a> proves the finite matrix inequalities; Section <a href="#sec:heat" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:heat">4</a> develops their Gaussian heat flow. Section <a href="#sec:smooth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:smooth">5</a> proves the repeated-mask estimate for smooth annuli. Sections <a href="#sec:frequency" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:frequency">6</a> and <a href="#sec:rough" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:rough">7</a> establish the uniform frequency-block estimate and control the hard endpoint errors. Section <a href="#sec:completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:completion">8</a> proves Theorems <a href="#thm:count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:count">2</a> and <a href="#thm:variation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:variation">1</a> by linearization, density, and rank summation. Section <a href="#sec:consequences" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:consequences">9</a> derives the maximal, principal-value, and scalar conclusions.

<a id="sec:preliminaries"></a>
### Truncations, coordinates, and maximal averages

We first fix the representatives of all finite truncations and the coordinate convention for their linearizations. We also record the one-dimensional maximal estimates that will control changes of the matrix energy. Constants denoted by $C$ may change from line to line; their additional dependences are indicated explicitly. For an integrable function $f$ and $L>0$, set $$\mathop{\mathrm{Dil}}_Lf(t)=L^{-1}f(t/L).$$ All inputs and matrices may be complex. Scalar forms are complex multilinear, so pairings with dual inputs do not include a conjugate.

<a id="stmt-lem-finite-truncations"></a>
<div id="lem:finite-truncations" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:finite-annulus-bound"></a>
**Lemma 3** (Finite truncations). *For $F,G\in L^3(\mathbb R^2)$, all finite annular integrals defining $B_{\varepsilon,R}(F,G)$ are absolutely convergent outside one common null set. On its complement they depend continuously on $(\varepsilon,R)$ in $0<\varepsilon<R<\infty$. Changing representatives changes this family only on a common null set. Moreover, $$\label{eq:finite-annulus-bound}
 \left\|\int_{\varepsilon<|t|<R}
 |F(x+t,y)G(x,y+t)|\,\frac{\,\mathrm dt}{|t|}\right\|_{3/2}
 \le 2\log(R/\varepsilon)\|F\|_3\|G\|_3.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-finite-truncations"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-finite-truncations</summary>

*Proof.* For each fixed $t$, Hölder’s inequality and translation invariance bound the $L^{3/2}$ norm of the product by $\|F\|_3\|G\|_3$. Minkowski’s integral inequality gives <a href="#eq:finite-annulus-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-annulus-bound">[eq:finite-annulus-bound]</a>. Apply this estimate to the countably many annuli $1/m<|t|<m$, $m\ge2$. Their union of exceptional null sets works for every finite annulus. Absolute continuity of the integral gives endpoint continuity. The same estimate applied to a zero $L^3$ difference proves the assertion about representatives, first on those countably many annuli and hence on all finite annuli. ◻

</details>

In particular, suprema over rational endpoint pairs agree with suprema over all endpoint pairs whenever only finitely many endpoints occur in a choice. Rational exhaustion makes the variation and the count quantities in the introduction measurable.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### The triangular coordinates

<a id="eq:pair-maps"></a>
<a id="eq:triangular-form"></a>
For most of the proof the inputs are smooth and compactly supported. The change of variables $$x=-a,\qquad y=-b,\qquad t=a+b+c$$ has absolute determinant one. Define $$\label{eq:pair-maps}
 G_1(b,c)=F(b+c,-b),\qquad G_2(c,a)=G(-a,c+a).$$ Both pair maps preserve $L^3$ norms. With a dual input $G_0(a,b)$, the expressions to be estimated take the form $$\label{eq:triangular-form}
 \Lambda_{\mathcal K}(G_0,G_1,G_2)
 =\iiint G_0(a,b)G_1(b,c)G_2(c,a)
       \mathcal K_{a,b}(a+b+c)\,\mathrm da\,\mathrm db\,\mathrm dc.$$ The maps in <a href="#eq:pair-maps" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-maps">[eq:pair-maps]</a> are invertible, so estimates for arbitrary smooth compactly supported $G_v$ are equivalent to the corresponding dual estimates in the original coordinates.

A *step choice* is a choice from a finite menu that is constant on each cell of a partition of $\mathbb R^2$ by finitely many horizontal and vertical lines. Unbounded cells are allowed. For annular linearizations, each menu item is a list of at most $n$ disjoint radius intervals $(\varepsilon_j,R_j)$, together with coefficients $|\beta_j|\le1$. At a fixed output point the intervals are disjoint up to endpoints. We first prove all estimates for step choices. Values on cell boundaries can be assigned any menu item; they do not affect the integrals.

For such choices and smooth compactly supported inputs, finite-mesh estimates pass to <a href="#eq:triangular-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:triangular-form">[eq:triangular-form]</a> by ordinary Riemann sums. Indeed, choose $T$ so large that all coordinates in the input supports lie in $[-T,T]$, put $h=T/N$, and sample at the nodes $u_i=ih$. After any auxiliary frequency integration over a fixed bounded block, the finitely many kernels used below are continuous in their scalar variable. The resulting integrands on $[-T,T]^3$ are bounded and continuous off finitely many coordinate planes. Their Riemann sums converge, as do the discrete $L^3$ norm sums. No limit of matrix energies is taken.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Maximal averages on a line

For functions on $\mathbb R$ and sequences on $\mathbb Z$, respectively, let $$M_{\mathbb R}f(x)=\sup_{r>0}\frac1{2r}\int_{x-r}^{x+r}|f(t)|\,\mathrm dt,
 \qquad
 M_{\mathbb Z}f(j)=\sup_{r\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}}
       \frac1{2r+1}\sum_{|m-j|\le r}|f(m)|.$$ The discrete operator includes singleton averages. We use these operators on individual coordinates of a function or array as well.

<a id="stmt-lem-line-maximal"></a>
<div id="lem:line-maximal" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:discrete-gaussian-maximal"></a>
<a id="eq:continuous-gaussian-maximal"></a>
**Lemma 4** (Line maximal estimates). *On either space, the centered maximal operator satisfies $$\mu\{Mf>\rho\}\le\frac3\rho\|f\|_1,
 \qquad
 \|Mf\|_p^p\le\frac{6p\,2^{p-1}}{p-1}\|f\|_p^p
 \quad(1<p<\infty).$$ Let $g_\sigma(t)=(2\pi\sigma)^{-1/2}e^{-t^2/(2\sigma)}$. There is an absolute constant $C$ such that $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_m h g_\sigma((m-j)h)|f(m)|
   &\le C M_{\mathbb Z}f(j), &&\sigma\ge h^2,
   \label{eq:discrete-gaussian-maximal}\\
 \int g_\sigma(t)|f(x-t)|\,\mathrm dt
   &\le C M_{\mathbb R}f(x), &&\sigma>0.
   \label{eq:continuous-gaussian-maximal}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-line-maximal"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-line-maximal</summary>

*Proof.* We recall the covering proof of the Hardy–Littlewood estimates (Hardy and Littlewood 1930) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[HardyLittlewood1930](#ref-HardyLittlewood1930)]. From any finite family of intervals, repeatedly select one of largest radius and discard those meeting it. The selected intervals are disjoint, and the union of the family lies in their triple-radius enlargements. Each enlargement has at most three times the original measure, also for integer intervals. Apply this to witnessing intervals centered at a finite subset of a discrete level set, or to a finite witnessing cover of a compact subset of a continuous level set. The latter level set is open because fixed-radius averages are continuous. Taking suprema over the finite or compact subsets proves the weak bound.

For $f\in L^p$, split at height $\rho/2$. The low part has maximal function at most $\rho/2$, while the high part is integrable. Thus $$\mu\{Mf>\rho\}
 \le\frac6\rho\int_{|f|>\rho/2}|f|\,\mathrm d\mu.$$ The distribution formula and Tonelli’s theorem give the strong bound after integrating $p\rho^{p-1}\,\mathrm d\rho$. Truncation of the distribution integral first makes this argument independent of any a priori finiteness assertion.

For <a href="#eq:discrete-gaussian-maximal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:discrete-gaussian-maximal">[eq:discrete-gaussian-maximal]</a>, put $a=\sqrt\sigma/h\ge1$. The kernel is $(\sqrt{2\pi}a)^{-1}e^{-(m-j)^2/(2a^2)}$. Split the sum into $|m-j|\le a$ and the annuli $2^{l-1}a<|m-j|\le2^la$, $l\ge1$. The sum of $|f|$ in the corresponding ball is at most $3\cdot2^la M_{\mathbb Z}f(j)$. The resulting series is bounded by a constant times $1+\sum_{l\ge1}2^l e^{-4^{l-1}/2}$. The same shell argument with integrals proves <a href="#eq:continuous-gaussian-maximal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:continuous-gaussian-maximal">[eq:continuous-gaussian-maximal]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:matrix"></a>
### Dimension-free matrix inequalities

The finite-dimensional part of the argument has two tasks: to bound a cyclic product by positive quadratic costs, and to keep those costs under control when matrices change or are averaged. We give all the needed estimates here, including the support and complex-phase arguments required for arbitrary complex input matrices. The averaging inequality in Lemma <a href="#lem:joint-convexity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:joint-convexity">8</a> will permit a Gaussian window to be replaced by a probability average of narrower windows without paying for their relative widths.

<a id="eq:row-star"></a>
<a id="eq:star-grams"></a>
All spaces in this section are finite-dimensional complex Hilbert spaces. Matrix inequalities mean inequalities of Hermitian quadratic forms. Set $$\Phi(P)=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(P^{3/2}),\qquad \mathop{\mathrm{supp}}P=(\ker P)^\perp
 \quad(P\ge0).$$ The application uses three spaces $H_v$, indexed by $v\in\mathbb Z/3\mathbb Z$, and maps $W_v:H_{v+1}\to H_v$. The two matrices incident to vertex $v$ form its *row star* $$\label{eq:row-star}
 R_v=[\,W_v\quad W_{v-1}^*\,]:
 H_{v+1}\oplus H_{v-1}\longrightarrow H_v.$$ We use both Gram matrices $$\label{eq:star-grams}
 T_v=R_v^*R_v,\qquad S_v=R_vR_v^*.$$ Their nonzero eigenvalues agree, including multiplicities, so $\Phi(T_v)=\Phi(S_v)$. Our eventual objective in this section is to bound the cyclic trace formed from the $W_v$ by second-derivative costs of these three energies. We first develop those costs for an arbitrary positive matrix $P$.

A matrix is *supported on $P$* if it vanishes on $\ker P$ on both sides. The Hilbert–Schmidt inner product is $\langle K,L\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(K^*L)$. Write $\mathsf L_A(X)=AX$ and $\mathsf R_A(X)=XA$. For Hermitian matrices $K,L$ supported on $P$, define <a id="eq:hessian-form"></a>$$\label{eq:hessian-form}
 \mathcal B_P(K,L)=\frac32\operatorname{Re}
 \left\langle K,
 (\mathsf L_{\sqrt P}+\mathsf R_{\sqrt P})^{-1}L
 \right\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}},
 \qquad b_P(K)=\mathcal B_P(K,K).$$ The inverse is taken on matrices acting on $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}P$, where the operator is positive definite. If $P=0$, its only supported direction is zero and these expressions are zero.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### The energy and its quadratic costs

<a id="stmt-lem-hessian"></a>
<div id="lem:hessian" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:hessian-metric"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Supported Hessian). *On the cone of matrices positive definite on a fixed support, $\Phi$ is smooth and $$d\Phi_P(K)=\frac32\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(\sqrt P K),\qquad
 d^2\Phi_P(K,L)=\mathcal B_P(K,L).$$ In an orthonormal eigenbasis for the positive eigenvalues $p_i$ of $P$, $$\label{eq:hessian-metric}
 b_P(K)=\frac32\sum_{i,j}
 \frac{|K_{ij}|^2}{\sqrt{p_i}+\sqrt{p_j}}.$$ In particular, $\mathcal B_P$ is a positive definite symmetric bilinear form on the real space of supported Hermitian directions, and $$|\mathcal B_P(K,L)|\le b_P(K)^{1/2}b_P(L)^{1/2}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-hessian"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-hessian</summary>

*Proof.* Restrict to the support and put $A=\sqrt P$. The derivative of the squaring map at $A$ is $X\mapsto AX+XA$; in an eigenbasis, it multiplies entry $(i,j)$ by $\sqrt{p_i}+\sqrt{p_j}>0$. The inverse function theorem gives a smooth positive square root, whose derivative $X_K$ solves $$AX_K+X_KA=K.$$ Multiplying by $A$ and taking traces gives $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(PX_K)=\tfrac12\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(AK)$. Hence differentiation of $\Phi(P)=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(P\sqrt P)$ yields $d\Phi_P(K)=\tfrac32\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(AK)$. Differentiating once more proves the Hessian formula. Formula <a href="#eq:hessian-metric" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hessian-metric">[eq:hessian-metric]</a> follows by diagonalizing $A$, and proves the remaining assertions. This is the square-root case of the classical divided-difference calculus (Daletskii 1957, sec. 1) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Daletskii1957](#ref-Daletskii1957)]. ◻

</details>

Both $\Phi$ and $\mathcal B$ are invariant under simultaneous unitary conjugation of their arguments. They are also invariant under extension of every argument by zero: their positive eigenvalues and supported entries do not change. These facts will let us compare Grams on different subspaces.

<a id="stmt-lem-order"></a>
<div id="lem:order" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:matrix-order"></a>
**Lemma 6** (Order comparison). *If $P,Q\ge0$ on the same space, $a\ge1$, and $P\le aQ$, then every Hermitian $K$ supported on $P$ is supported on $Q$, and $$\label{eq:matrix-order}
 b_P(K)\ge a^{-1/2}b_Q(K).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-order"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-order</summary>

*Proof.* For $x\in\ker Q$ we have $0\le\langle Px,x\rangle
\le a\langle Qx,x\rangle=0$, so $\ker Q\subseteq\ker P$. This proves the support assertion.

We recall the order facts used in the proof. Inversion reverses order for positive definite matrices, as follows by conjugating $0<X\le Y$ by $X^{-1/2}$, inverting, and conjugating back. Also $$\sqrt X=\frac1\pi\int_0^\infty
 X(X+tI)^{-1}\,\frac{\,\mathrm dt}{\sqrt t}\qquad(X\ge0).$$ Spectral calculus reduces this norm-convergent formula to the scalar integral. Since $X(X+tI)^{-1}=I-t(X+tI)^{-1}$, inversion order shows that square root preserves order. This is the classical Loewner–Heinz square-root monotonicity; see, for example, Kwong (Kwong 1975, Theorem 1 and Section 2) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Kwong1975](#ref-Kwong1975)]. The supported Hessian comparison below is derived from these order facts here.

For $\eta>0$, $$P+\eta I\le a(Q+\eta I),\qquad
 \sqrt{P+\eta I}\le\sqrt a\sqrt{Q+\eta I}.$$ If $A\le B$, then $\mathsf L_A+\mathsf R_A\le\mathsf L_B+\mathsf R_B$ on Hilbert–Schmidt space: the difference has quadratic form $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(X^*(B-A)X)+\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(X^*X(B-A))\ge0$. Invert these positive operators to obtain $$b_{P+\eta I}(K)\ge a^{-1/2}b_{Q+\eta I}(K).$$ Let $\eta\downarrow0$. In an eigenbasis, every entry of $K$ meeting the kernel of the corresponding limiting matrix is zero. Thus Formula <a href="#eq:hessian-metric" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hessian-metric">[eq:hessian-metric]</a> gives precisely the supported costs on both sides of <a href="#eq:matrix-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-order">[eq:matrix-order]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-insertion"></a>
<div id="lem:insertion" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (Inserted Gram bound). *For a linear map $R:E\to F$ and a Hermitian map $L:F\to F$, the matrix $R^*LR$ is supported on $R^*R$, and <a id="eq:insertion-bound"></a>$$\label{eq:insertion-bound}
 b_{R^*R}(R^*LR)
 \le\frac34\lVert L\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^2\Phi(R^*R).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-insertion"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-insertion</summary>

*Proof.* The insertion is Hermitian and annihilates $\ker R=\ker(R^*R)$. If $R=0$, there is nothing to prove. Otherwise let $\rho_i>0$ be the positive singular values of $R$, and let $C$ be the compression of $L$ to the corresponding left singular vectors. Then $\lVert C\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\le\lVert L\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}$ and $$b_{R^*R}(R^*LR)
 =\frac32\sum_{i,j}
 \frac{\rho_i^2\rho_j^2}{\rho_i+\rho_j}|C_{ij}|^2.$$ For $x,y>0$, $x^2y^2/(x+y)\le (xy)^{3/2}/2\le(x^3+y^3)/4$. Every row and column of $C$ has squared Euclidean norm at most $\lVert L\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^2$. Therefore $$b_{R^*R}(R^*LR)
 \le\frac38\sum_{i,j}(\rho_i^3+\rho_j^3)|C_{ij}|^2
 \le\frac34\lVert L\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^2\sum_i\rho_i^3,$$ as required. ◻

</details>

We next show that averaging a Gram and its inserted direction can only decrease the corresponding cost. No uniform lower bound on the positive eigenvalues is required.

<a id="stmt-lem-joint-convexity"></a>
<div id="lem:joint-convexity" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8** (Joint convexity under probability averages). *Let $(\Omega,\nu)$ be a probability space and $E$ a fixed subspace of a finite-dimensional complex Hilbert space. Suppose $T_\omega\ge0$ and $K_\omega=K_\omega^*$ are measurable matrices, $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}T_\omega=E$ and $K_\omega$ is supported on $T_\omega$ almost everywhere, and $$\int_\Omega\bigl(\lVert T_\omega\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}
                  +\lVert K_\omega\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\bigr)\,\,\mathrm d\nu(\omega)
 <\infty.$$ Then $\overline T=\int T_\omega\,\,\mathrm d\nu$ has support $E$, $\overline K=\int K_\omega\,\,\mathrm d\nu$ is supported on $\overline T$, and <a id="eq:joint-convexity"></a>$$\label{eq:joint-convexity}
 b_{\overline T}(\overline K)
 \le\int_\Omega b_{T_\omega}(K_\omega)\,\,\mathrm d\nu(\omega).$$ The right side may be infinite.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-joint-convexity"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-joint-convexity</summary>

*Proof.* If $E=\{0\}$, all matrices and costs vanish. Otherwise restrict every matrix to $E$. For every nonzero $x\in E$, the positive quantity $\langle T_\omega x,x\rangle$ has positive integral, so $\overline T$ is positive definite on $E$. All averages vanish on $E^\perp$, proving the support assertions.

<a id="eq:square-root-average"></a>
Put $A_\omega=\sqrt{T_\omega}$ and $A=\int A_\omega\,\,\mathrm d\nu$. The integral exists by the stated integrability and Cauchy–Schwarz. For every vector $x$, the vector-valued Cauchy–Schwarz inequality gives $$\lVert Ax\rVert^2
 \le\int\lVert A_\omega x\rVert^2\,\,\mathrm d\nu
 =\langle\overline T x,x\rangle.$$ Consequently $A^2\le\overline T$, and square-root order, proved in Lemma <a href="#lem:order" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:order">6</a>, implies $$\label{eq:square-root-average}
 \sqrt{\overline T}\ge\int\sqrt{T_\omega}\,\,\mathrm d\nu.$$ For a positive definite selfadjoint operator $\mathsf M$ on a real Hilbert space, completing the square proves <a id="eq:inverse-variational"></a>$$\label{eq:inverse-variational}
 \langle K,\mathsf M^{-1}K\rangle
 =\sup_X\{2\langle K,X\rangle-\langle X,\mathsf M X\rangle\}.$$ Apply this identity on Hermitian matrices with real Hilbert–Schmidt inner product. The operators $\mathsf L_{\sqrt T}+\mathsf R_{\sqrt T}$ preserve that real space. By <a href="#eq:square-root-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:square-root-average">[eq:square-root-average]</a>, for each Hermitian $X$ the expression inside the supremum for $(\overline T,\overline K)$ is at most the average of the corresponding expressions for $(T_\omega,K_\omega)$. Taking the supremum and then bounding the supremum of an integral by the integral of the pointwise suprema gives $$b_{\overline T}(\overline K)
 \le\int b_{T_\omega}(K_\omega)\,\,\mathrm d\nu.$$ Every inverse in this argument is taken on matrices acting on $E$; uniform invertibility over $\omega$ was never assumed. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Changes that alter the rank

The Hessian was defined on a fixed support, but switching matrix entries on or off can change that support. The following first-derivative formula remains valid across such changes. It is a special case of the gradient formula for unitarily invariant matrix functions (Lewis 1995, Theorem 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Lewis1995](#ref-Lewis1995)].

<a id="stmt-lem-rank-change"></a>
<div id="lem:rank-change" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:rank-change-derivative"></a>
<a id="eq:entry-gradient"></a>
**Lemma 9** (Rank changes and entrywise derivatives). *For rectangular complex matrices $R_-,R_+$ of the same size, set $R(z)=(1-z)R_-+zR_+$ and $D=R_+-R_-$. Then $$\label{eq:rank-change-derivative}
 \Phi(R_+^*R_+)-\Phi(R_-^*R_-)
 =3\int_0^1\operatorname{Re}
 \langle R(z)\sqrt{R(z)^*R(z)},D\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}\,\,\mathrm dz.$$ For every rectangular matrix $R$ and every entry $(i,j)$, $$\label{eq:entry-gradient}
 |(R\sqrt{R^*R})_{ij}|
 \le\lVert\operatorname{row}_iR\rVert_2
       \lVert\operatorname{col}_jR\rVert_2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-rank-change"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-rank-change</summary>

*Proof.* For $\eta>0$, Lemma <a href="#lem:hessian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hessian">5</a> gives $$\frac{\,\mathrm d}{\,\mathrm dz}\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}((R(z)^*R(z)+\eta I)^{3/2})
 =3\operatorname{Re}
 \langle R(z)\sqrt{R(z)^*R(z)+\eta I},D\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}.$$ Spectral calculus implies $\lVert\sqrt{R^*R+\eta I}-\sqrt{R^*R}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\le\sqrt\eta$. The matrices $R(z)$ stay bounded for $0\le z\le1$, so the derivatives converge uniformly as $\eta\downarrow0$, and the endpoint energies converge. Integrating first and passing to the limit proves <a href="#eq:rank-change-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rank-change-derivative">[eq:rank-change-derivative]</a>.

For <a href="#eq:entry-gradient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entry-gradient">[eq:entry-gradient]</a>, Cauchy–Schwarz in the matrix product bounds the entry by the norm of row $i$ of $R$ times the norm of column $j$ of $\sqrt{R^*R}$. The latter column has squared norm $(R^*R)_{jj}$, also the squared norm of column $j$ of $R$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### The cyclic product

Return to the three maps $W_v$ and the row stars <a href="#eq:row-star" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:row-star">[eq:row-star]</a>–<a href="#eq:star-grams" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:star-grams">[eq:star-grams]</a>. The reason for combining the two incident matrices is the following estimate: three inserted star costs control the entire cyclic product.

<a id="stmt-lem-mixed-trace"></a>
<div id="lem:mixed-trace" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:mixed-trace"></a>
**Lemma 10** (Mixed trace inequality). *For Hermitian maps $D_v:H_v\to H_v$, put $D_* =\max_v\lVert D_v\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}$. Then $$\label{eq:mixed-trace}
 \left|\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(D_0W_0D_1W_1D_2W_2)\right|
 \le\frac53D_*\sum_{v=0}^2b_{T_v}(R_v^*D_vR_v).$$ The constant is independent of the dimensions and no Gram is assumed invertible.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-mixed-trace"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-mixed-trace</summary>

*Proof.* We put the three costs in one quadratic form, bound a cubic trace, and then recover the complex phase of the cyclic product.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
###### One quadratic form.

On $H=H_0\oplus H_1\oplus H_2$, define $$M=\begin{pmatrix}
 0&W_0&W_2^*\\
 W_0^*&0&W_1\\
 W_2&W_1^*&0
 \end{pmatrix},\qquad
 D=\operatorname{diag}(D_0,D_1,D_2).$$ Both are Hermitian and $\lVert D\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=D_*$. If $P_v$ is the orthogonal projection onto $H_v$, put $A_v=MP_vM$ and $K_v=MP_vDM$. After permuting blocks and extending by zero, these are respectively $T_v$ and $R_v^*D_vR_v$. The matrix $K_v$ is Hermitian because $P_v$ commutes with $D$. Moreover, $$\ker A_v=\ker(P_vM)\subseteq\ker K_v,
 \qquad 0\le A_v\le M^2.$$ Thus $K_v$ is supported on $A_v$ and on $M^2$. Lemma <a href="#lem:order" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:order">6</a> and the inequality $\sum_{v=0}^2 b(X_v)\ge\tfrac13 b(\sum_vX_v)$ for a positive quadratic form give <a id="eq:trace-common-metric"></a>$$\label{eq:trace-common-metric}
 \mathcal E:=\sum_v b_{T_v}(R_v^*D_vR_v)
 \ge\sum_v b_{M^2}(K_v)
 \ge\frac13 b_{M^2}(MDM).$$

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
###### A cubic trace estimate.

<a id="eq:trace-Q"></a>
Choose an orthonormal eigenbasis of $M$, with real eigenvalues $m_i$ ordered so that $q_i=|m_i|$ is nonincreasing. Ties are arbitrary. In that basis Formula <a href="#eq:hessian-metric" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hessian-metric">[eq:hessian-metric]</a> yields $$\label{eq:trace-Q}
 \mathcal E\ge\frac12Q,\qquad
 Q:=\sum_{i,j:q_i+q_j>0}
 \frac{q_i^2q_j^2}{q_i+q_j}|D_{ij}|^2.$$ Any term with one zero $q_i$ is zero; pairs with both zero are omitted. Write $$A_i=q_i^3|D_{ii}|^2,\quad
 E_i=q_i\sum_{j>i}q_j^2|D_{ij}|^2,\quad
 A=\sum_iA_i,\quad E=\sum_iE_i.$$ Hermitian symmetry and $q_j\le q_i$ for $j>i$ imply <a id="eq:trace-AE"></a>$$\label{eq:trace-AE}
 Q=\frac A2+
 2\sum_{\substack{i<j\\q_i+q_j>0}}
 \frac{q_i^2q_j^2}{q_i+q_j}|D_{ij}|^2
 \ge\frac A2+E.$$ Expand $$\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}((DM)^3)=\sum_{i,j,k}m_im_jm_kD_{ij}D_{jk}D_{ki}$$ and group triples by the multiplicity of their smallest index $i$. If it occurs once, its three cyclic positions give $$3m_i\sum_{j,k>i}m_jm_kD_{ij}D_{jk}D_{ki}.$$ The inner sum is $x^*D_{>i}x$ for $x=(m_jD_{ji})_{j>i}$, where $D_{>i}$ is the corresponding compression of $D$. Since $\lVert D_{>i}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\le D_*$, this contribution has modulus at most $3D_*E_i$. The argument allows $j=k$ and retains the signs of the $m_j$.

<a id="eq:trace-cubic"></a>
If the smallest index occurs twice, its contribution is $$3m_i^2D_{ii}\sum_{k>i}m_k|D_{ik}|^2.$$ By Cauchy–Schwarz and $\sum_k|D_{ik}|^2=\lVert De_i\rVert^2\le D_*^2$, $$\left|\sum_{k>i}m_k|D_{ik}|^2\right|
 \le D_*\left(\sum_{k>i}q_k^2|D_{ik}|^2\right)^{1/2}.$$ The contribution is therefore at most $3D_*\sqrt{A_iE_i}$, also when $q_i=0$. Finally, if all three indices equal $i$, the modulus is at most $D_*A_i$. These cases exhaust all triples. Summing and using Cauchy–Schwarz gives $$\label{eq:trace-cubic}
 \begin{aligned}
 |\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}((DM)^3)|
 &\le D_*\bigl(A+3E+3\sqrt{AE}\bigr)\\
 &\le D_*\left(\frac52A+\frac92E\right)
 \le5D_*\left(\frac A2+E\right)
 \le5D_*Q.
 \end{aligned}$$

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
###### Recovering the complex phase.

Set $\tau=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(D_0W_0D_1W_1D_2W_2)$. Since $M$ has zero diagonal blocks, every nonzero closed three-step block product visits all three vertices. The three starting vertices in one orientation give $\tau$, by cyclicity of trace; the reverse orientation gives $\overline\tau$, because the $D_v$ are Hermitian. Consequently $$\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}((DM)^3)=6\operatorname{Re}\tau.$$ When $\tau\ne0$, choose $|z|=1$ so that $z\tau=|\tau|$ and replace $W_0$ by $zW_0$. This changes the stars by $$R_0\longmapsto R_0\operatorname{diag}(zI_{H_1},I_{H_2}),\qquad
 R_1\longmapsto R_1\operatorname{diag}(I_{H_2},\overline zI_{H_0}),$$ and leaves $R_2$ unchanged. These right multipliers are unitary; each conjugates the Gram and its insertion by the same unitary. Thus $\mathcal E$ and $D_*$ are unchanged. Apply <a href="#eq:trace-Q" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trace-Q">[eq:trace-Q]</a> and <a href="#eq:trace-cubic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trace-cubic">[eq:trace-cubic]</a> to the modified matrices, with their own value of $Q$, to obtain $$6|\tau|\le5D_*Q\le10D_*\mathcal E.$$ This is <a href="#eq:mixed-trace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mixed-trace">[eq:mixed-trace]</a>. The case $\tau=0$ is immediate. ◻

</details>

In the applications a diagonal insertion may be complex. Write $D_v=D_v^{(0)}+iD_v^{(1)}$ for its real and imaginary diagonal parts, and put $D_* =\max_v\lVert D_v\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}$. Expanding gives eight products with Hermitian diagonals, each bounded by Lemma <a href="#lem:mixed-trace" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:mixed-trace">10</a>. Both parts have operator norm at most $D_*$, and each of their six costs occurs four times in the sum. Thus <a id="eq:complex-mixed-trace"></a>$$\label{eq:complex-mixed-trace}
 \left|\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(D_0W_0D_1W_1D_2W_2)\right|
 \le\frac{20}{3}D_*\sum_{v=0}^2\sum_{\epsilon=0}^1
 b_{T_v}(R_v^*D_v^{(\epsilon)}R_v).$$ All directions appearing in this bound remain Hermitian.

<a id="sec:heat"></a>
### Gaussian heat dissipation

We use Gaussian weights to turn the matrix costs of the preceding section into derivatives of an energy. Integration over a plane of Gaussian centers then gives two complementary estimates: strict dissipation of the sum of the three star energies when the variances are comparable, and single-edge dissipation for arbitrary positive variance rates. Both estimates are uniform in the matrix dimension. We retain arbitrary positive rates in the differential identities, since both forms of dissipation will be needed below. Continuous Gaussian telescoping for entangled forms also appears in Durcik (Durcik 2015, Lemma 3 and Section 3) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Durcik2015](#ref-Durcik2015)].

Fix $h>0$, an integer $N\ge0$, and nodes $u_i=ih$, $-N\le i\le N$. The vertex index $v$ is taken in $\mathbb Z/3\mathbb Z$. For finite complex edge arrays $A_v(i,j)$, put $$n_v=\left(\sum_{i,j=-N}^N h^2|A_v(i,j)|^3\right)^{1/3}.$$ For centers $p_v\in\mathbb R$ and variances $\sigma_v>0$, define <a id="eq:weights"></a>$$\label{eq:weights}
 \begin{gathered}
 g_\sigma(t)=(2\pi\sigma)^{-1/2}e^{-t^2/(2\sigma)},
 \qquad w_v(i)=h g_{\sigma_v}(u_i-p_v),\\
 W_v(i,j)=A_v(i,j)\sqrt{w_v(i)w_{v+1}(j)}.
 \end{gathered}$$ Form the stars and their Grams as in <a href="#eq:row-star" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:row-star">[eq:row-star]</a>–<a href="#eq:star-grams" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:star-grams">[eq:star-grams]</a>, and set $e_v=\Phi(T_v)=\Phi(S_v)$. To distinguish the two blocks of the star, write $$C_{v,v+1}=W_v,\qquad C_{v,v-1}=W_{v-1}^*,
 \qquad R_v=[C_{v,v+1}\ C_{v,v-1}].$$ The diagonal Gaussian scores and the corresponding inserted Grams are $$N_v=\operatorname{diag}_i\frac{u_i-p_v}{\sigma_v},\qquad
 U_v=R_v^*N_vR_v,\qquad
 P_{v,w}=C_{v,w}N_wC_{v,w}^*\quad(w\ne v).$$ Their quadratic costs are <a id="eq:heat-costs"></a>$$\label{eq:heat-costs}
 V_v=b_{T_v}(U_v),\qquad
 Y_{v,w}=b_{S_v}(P_{v,w}),\qquad
 Z_v=\mathcal B_{S_v}(P_{v,v+1},P_{v,v-1}).$$ All these insertions are supported on their respective Grams: an inserted Gram $B^*DB$ annihilates $\ker B$ on both sides.

For $d\in\mathbb R$, equip the plane $\Pi_d=\{p_0+p_1+p_2=d\}$ with the measure <a id="eq:heat-plane-measure"></a>$$\label{eq:heat-plane-measure}
 \int_{\Pi_d}H\,\,\mathrm d\pi_d
 =\int_{\mathbb R^2}H(p_0,p_1,d-p_0-p_1)\,\,\mathrm dp_0\,\,\mathrm dp_1.$$ Any pair of centers can be the free coordinates, since the changes between these charts have absolute determinant one. Given positive rates $\boldsymbol\alpha=(\alpha_0,\alpha_1,\alpha_2)$, we evaluate the weights at $\sigma_i=\alpha_i s$ and write $$J_v(d,s)=\int_{\Pi_d}e_v\,\,\mathrm d\pi_d,
 \qquad J(d,s)=\sum_vJ_v(d,s),
 \qquad \Sigma_\alpha=\sum_i\alpha_i.$$ The arrays remain fixed whenever a scale derivative is taken.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Differentiation and the plane identities

<a id="stmt-lem-heat-identities"></a>
<div id="lem:heat-identities" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:heat-identity"></a>
<a id="eq:heat-mixed"></a>
<a id="eq:heat-plane-identities"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Heat identities). *For fixed finite arrays and arbitrary positive rates, the energies $e_v$ are smooth in the centers and positive variances. For $s>0$, $$\begin{aligned}
 2\partial_s e_v
 &=\sum_i\alpha_i\partial_{p_i}^2e_v
   -\alpha_vV_v-\sum_{w\ne v}\alpha_wY_{v,w},
   \label{eq:heat-identity}\\
 \partial_{p_{v+1}}\partial_{p_{v-1}}e_v&=Z_v.
   \label{eq:heat-mixed}
\end{aligned}$$ The plane integrals are finite, continuously differentiable in $s$, and twice continuously differentiable in $d$, with $$\label{eq:heat-plane-identities}
 \begin{aligned}
 2\partial_sJ_v(d,s)
 &=\Sigma_\alpha\partial_d^2J_v(d,s)
   -\int_{\Pi_d}\left(\alpha_vV_v+
                  \sum_{w\ne v}\alpha_wY_{v,w}\right)\,\mathrm d\pi_d,\\
 \partial_d^2J_v(d,s)&=\int_{\Pi_d}Z_v\,\,\mathrm d\pi_d.
 \end{aligned}$$ On compact positive scale intervals, the plane integrals of the absolute values of all derivatives used here grow at most polynomially in $|d|$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-heat-identities"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-heat-identities</summary>

*Proof.* We first check that the possible kernels of the Grams cause no differentiability problem. Each star has the form $$R_v=D_{\rm row}R_v^0D_{\rm col},\qquad
 R_v^0=[A_v\ A_{v-1}^*],$$ where both diagonal multipliers are positive and invertible. With the column parameters fixed, $\ker R_v$ is fixed, so $T_v$ has fixed support as the row parameters vary. With the row parameters fixed, $\operatorname{ran}R_v$ is fixed, so $S_v$ has fixed support as either or both column parameters vary. For joint smoothness, factor a nonzero $R_v^0$ as $XY^*$ with $X,Y$ of full column rank. Set $X'=D_{\rm row}X$, $Y'=D_{\rm col}Y$, $P'=X'^*X'$, and $Q'=Y'^*Y'$. These last two matrices are positive definite and smooth, and the nonzero eigenvalues of $R_vR_v^*$ are those of $Q'^{1/2}P'Q'^{1/2}$. Consequently $$e_v=\Phi(Q'^{1/2}P'Q'^{1/2})$$ is smooth. The rank-zero case is immediate.

For one Gaussian density $w=h g_\sigma(u-p)$ and its score $N=(u-p)/\sigma$, $$\partial_pw=Nw,\qquad
 \partial_p^2w=(N^2-\sigma^{-1})w,\qquad
 2\partial_\sigma w=\partial_p^2w.$$ With the columns fixed, $T_v$ is linear in the row density, and hence $$\partial_{p_v}T_v=U_v,\qquad
 2\partial_{\sigma_v}T_v=\partial_{p_v}^2T_v.$$ The chain rule on its fixed support and Lemma <a href="#lem:hessian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hessian">5</a> give $$2\partial_{\sigma_v}e_v=\partial_{p_v}^2e_v-V_v.$$ For a column vertex $w\ne v$, use $S_v$ instead. It is linear in that column density, has fixed support, and satisfies $\partial_{p_w}S_v=P_{v,w}$. Thus $2\partial_{\sigma_w}e_v=\partial_{p_w}^2e_v-Y_{v,w}$. Taking the variance derivative along $\sigma_i=\alpha_i s$ proves <a href="#eq:heat-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-identity">[eq:heat-identity]</a>. The two summands of $S_v=C_{v,v+1}C_{v,v+1}^*+C_{v,v-1}C_{v,v-1}^*$ depend on different column densities. Its mixed derivative in those two centers is zero; the Hessian term in the chain rule is therefore exactly $Z_v$, proving <a href="#eq:heat-mixed" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-mixed">[eq:heat-mixed]</a>.

Before integrating these identities, we give the domination needed for differentiation under the integral. Lemma <a href="#lem:insertion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:insertion">7</a> yields $$V_v\le\tfrac34\|N_v\|_{\mathrm{op}}^2e_v,
 \qquad
 Y_{v,w}\le\tfrac34\|N_w\|_{\mathrm{op}}^2e_v,
 \qquad
 2|Z_v|\le Y_{v,v+1}+Y_{v,v-1}.$$ For the second inequality, apply the insertion bound to $R_v^*$, padding $N_w$ by zero on the other column block. For any Hermitian $B$ on the row space, $$\big|\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(\sqrt{T_v}R_v^*BR_v)\big|
 \le\|B\|_{\mathrm{op}}e_v,$$ because $R_v\sqrt{T_v}R_v^*$ is positive and has trace $e_v$. The corresponding estimate for a column insertion follows by using $S_v$. The Gaussian derivative formulas and the Hessian chain rule therefore bound every first center derivative, pure second center derivative, mixed column derivative, and first scale derivative used above by a polynomial in the centers times $e_v$, locally uniformly in $s>0$.

<a id="eq:heat-energy-bound"></a>
The energy itself satisfies $$\label{eq:heat-energy-bound}
 e_v\le\left(\|W_v\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2+
                  \|W_{v-1}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\right)^{3/2}
 \le\sqrt2\left(\|W_v\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^3+
                       \|W_{v-1}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^3\right).$$ Since the node set is finite, each edge norm cubed is bounded by a Gaussian in its two endpoint centers, locally uniformly for positive variances. On $\Pi_d$, with $|d|\le D$, each pair of distinct indices $i,j$ satisfies $$p_0^2+p_1^2+p_2^2\le4(p_i^2+p_j^2)+3D^2.$$ Thus the preceding bounds have integrable majorants in every plane chart, locally uniformly in $(d,s)$. Dominated convergence justifies all the asserted derivatives. To obtain polynomial growth in $d$, use the two endpoints of each bounding Gaussian as free coordinates; the third center is $d$ minus their sum. Integrating a polynomial times that Gaussian leaves at most polynomial growth in $|d|$. Constants in these differentiability arguments may depend on the fixed arrays, mesh, rates, and compact scale interval, but do not enter the estimates below.

Choosing $p_i$ as the dependent coordinate gives, for each $i$, $$\partial_d^2J_v=\int_{\Pi_d}\partial_{p_i}^2e_v\,\,\mathrm d\pi_d.$$ To obtain the mixed derivative, first choose $p_{v+1}$ dependent and differentiate once. Reparametrize the resulting integral with $p_{v-1}$ dependent and differentiate once more. This gives $$\partial_d^2J_v
 =\int_{\Pi_d}\partial_{p_{v-1}}\partial_{p_{v+1}}e_v\,\,\mathrm d\pi_d
 =\int_{\Pi_d}Z_v\,\,\mathrm d\pi_d.$$ Integrating <a href="#eq:heat-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-identity">[eq:heat-identity]</a> proves the first identity in <a href="#eq:heat-plane-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-plane-identities">[eq:heat-plane-identities]</a>. These comparisons concern integrated derivatives; no pointwise equality of pure and mixed center derivatives is asserted. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Strict dissipation at comparable variances

The plane identities contain mixed terms $Z_v$ whose signs are not controlled. We bound them by column costs and then compare those costs with the row cost at the neighboring vertex. The resulting loss is $\sqrt2$, small enough to leave strict dissipation for the variance ratios we use.

<a id="stmt-lem-plane-dissipation"></a>
<div id="lem:plane-dissipation" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:heat-plane-dissipation"></a>
<a id="eq:heat-strict"></a>
**Lemma 12** (Plane dissipation). *For fixed finite arrays and arbitrary positive rates, $$\label{eq:heat-plane-dissipation}
 2\partial_sJ(d,s)\le\int_{\Pi_d}\sum_w
 \left[-\alpha_w+
 \sqrt2\max\{0,\Sigma_\alpha/2-\alpha_w\}\right]V_w\,\,\mathrm d\pi_d.$$ In particular, put $\lambda=11/10$. For each permutation of $(1,\lambda,\lambda)$, $$\label{eq:heat-strict}
 \begin{gathered}
 -\partial_sJ(d,s)\ge c_*\int_{\Pi_d}\sum_wV_w\,\,\mathrm d\pi_d,\\
 c_* =\tfrac12\min\{1-\tfrac35\sqrt2,
                      \tfrac{11}{10}-\tfrac12\sqrt2\}>0.
 \end{gathered}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-plane-dissipation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-plane-dissipation</summary>

<a id="eq:heat-neighbors"></a>
*Proof.* We first prove the neighbor comparison $$\label{eq:heat-neighbors}
 Y_{w+1,w}+Y_{w-1,w}\le\sqrt2\,V_w.$$ Write $A=W_w$, $B=W_{w-1}^*$, and $N=N_w$. The elementary inequality $\|Az+Bz'\|^2\le2\|Az\|^2+2\|Bz'\|^2$ gives $$T_w\le2Q,\qquad Q=\operatorname{diag}(A^*A,B^*B).$$ By Lemma <a href="#lem:order" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:order">6</a>, $V_w\ge2^{-1/2}b_Q([A\ B]^*N[A\ B])$. In a block-preserving eigenbasis of $Q$, the Hessian cost is a sum of nonnegative squared entries. Discarding the off-diagonal blocks gives $$b_Q([A\ B]^*N[A\ B])
 \ge b_{A^*A}(A^*NA)+b_{B^*B}(B^*NB).$$ Now $A^*A\le S_{w+1}$ and $B^*B\le S_{w-1}$; the respective insertions are $P_{w+1,w}$ and $P_{w-1,w}$. A second use of Lemma <a href="#lem:order" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:order">6</a>, with comparison factor one, proves <a href="#eq:heat-neighbors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-neighbors">[eq:heat-neighbors]</a>.

In <a href="#eq:heat-plane-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-plane-identities">[eq:heat-plane-identities]</a>, replace $2Z_v$ by its upper bound $Y_{v,v+1}+Y_{v,v-1}$ and sum over $v$. The two column costs carrying the score at vertex $w$ have common coefficient $\Sigma_\alpha/2-\alpha_w$. If this coefficient is negative, discard those terms; otherwise apply <a href="#eq:heat-neighbors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-neighbors">[eq:heat-neighbors]</a>. This proves <a href="#eq:heat-plane-dissipation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-plane-dissipation">[eq:heat-plane-dissipation]</a>. For the specified rates, $\Sigma_\alpha=16/5$, and the two possible coefficients are $-1+(3/5)\sqrt2$ and $-11/10+(1/2)\sqrt2$. Both are negative, giving <a href="#eq:heat-strict" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-strict">[eq:heat-strict]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Energy bounds and the single-edge case

The decrease in <a href="#eq:heat-strict" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-strict">[eq:heat-strict]</a> controls the accumulated costs by an initial energy. We now bound that energy uniformly once the Gaussian variances are no smaller than the mesh scale.

<a id="stmt-lem-initial-energy"></a>
<div id="lem:initial-energy" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:initial-energy"></a>
**Lemma 13** (Initial energy). *For all $d\in\mathbb R$ and all variances $\sigma_v\ge h^2$, $$\label{eq:initial-energy}
 \begin{gathered}
 \int_{\Pi_d}\|W_v\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^3\,\,\mathrm d\pi_d\le M n_v^3,
 \qquad M=1+\sqrt{2/\pi},\\
 \int_{\Pi_d}\sum_ve_v\,\,\mathrm d\pi_d
       \le2\sqrt2 M\sum_v n_v^3.
 \end{gathered}$$ These bounds also hold with the original right-hand sides if any entries are replaced by entries of smaller absolute value.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-initial-energy"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-initial-energy</summary>

*Proof.* The Gaussian grid mass obeys <a id="eq:heat-mesh-mass"></a>$$\label{eq:heat-mesh-mass}
 \sum_{i=-N}^Nh g_\sigma(u_i-p)
 \le1+\frac{2h}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma}}.$$ Indeed, for $u_i\ge p+h$ compare the summand with the integral over the preceding interval of length $h$; for $u_i\le p-h$, use the succeeding interval. These intervals are disjoint, and the at most two remaining nodes contribute at most $2h\sup g_\sigma$. For $\sigma\ge h^2$, the right side is at most $M$. Weighted Hölder therefore gives $$\|W_v\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^3
 =\left(\sum_{i,j}|A_v(i,j)|^2w_v(i)w_{v+1}(j)\right)^{3/2}
 \le M\sum_{i,j}|A_v(i,j)|^3w_v(i)w_{v+1}(j).$$ Use $p_v,p_{v+1}$ as free coordinates on $\Pi_d$. Each density integrates to $h$, proving the first estimate in <a href="#eq:initial-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:initial-energy">[eq:initial-energy]</a>. Each edge occurs in two stars, so <a href="#eq:heat-energy-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-energy-bound">[eq:heat-energy-bound]</a> proves the second. The same upper bounds decrease when the entrywise absolute values decrease. ◻

</details>

For example, if $h^2\le s_0<s_1$ and the rates are a permutation of $(1,\lambda,\lambda)$, these two lemmas imply <a id="eq:heat-fixed-array-integral"></a>$$\label{eq:heat-fixed-array-integral}
 \int_{s_0}^{s_1}\int_{\Pi_d}\sum_vV_v\,\,\mathrm d\pi_d\,\,\mathrm ds
 \le\frac{J(d,s_0)-J(d,s_1)}{c_*}
 \le\frac{2\sqrt2 M}{c_*}\sum_v n_v^3.$$ The first inequality retains the terminal energy, which is essential when arrays are changed at finitely many scales.

For much more unequal variance rates, <a href="#eq:heat-plane-dissipation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-plane-dissipation">[eq:heat-plane-dissipation]</a> does not guarantee that the summed star energy decreases. A single edge nevertheless has no mixed term and retains exact dissipation. We state that consequence separately.

<a id="stmt-lem-single-edge-energy"></a>
<div id="lem:single-edge-energy" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:heat-single-edge"></a>
<a id="eq:heat-single-edge-integral"></a>
**Lemma 14** (Single-edge energy). *Fix $v\ne w$, retain the weighted block $C=C_{v,w}$, and delete the other block of the star at $v$. For arbitrary positive rates, set $$\begin{aligned}
 e^e_{v,w}&=\Phi(CC^*),\qquad
 V^e_{v,w}=b_{C^*C}(C^*N_vC),\qquad
 Y^e_{v,w}=b_{CC^*}(CN_wC^*),\\
 J^e_{v,w}(s)&=\iint_{\mathbb R^2}e^e_{v,w}\,\,\mathrm dp_v\,\,\mathrm dp_w.
 \end{aligned}$$ The plane integrals of $e^e_{v,w}$, $V^e_{v,w}$, and $Y^e_{v,w}$ are independent of $d$. Moreover, $$\label{eq:heat-single-edge}
 -2\partial_sJ^e_{v,w}(s)
 =\iint_{\mathbb R^2}
      (\alpha_vV^e_{v,w}+\alpha_wY^e_{v,w})\,\,\mathrm dp_v\,\,\mathrm dp_w.$$ If $n_e$ denotes the discrete $L^3$ norm of this edge’s unweighted array, then, whenever $\alpha_vs_0,\alpha_ws_0\ge h^2$ and $s_1>s_0$, $$\label{eq:heat-single-edge-integral}
 \int_{s_0}^{s_1}\iint_{\mathbb R^2}
      (\alpha_vV^e_{v,w}+\alpha_wY^e_{v,w})
          \,\,\mathrm dp_v\,\,\mathrm dp_w\,\,\mathrm ds
 \le2M n_e^3.$$ For the original two-block star, $Y_{v,w}\le Y^e_{v,w}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-single-edge-energy"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-single-edge-energy</summary>

*Proof.* The three integrands depend only on the two endpoint centers. Using these as free coordinates proves independence of $d$. In the one-edge star the other column insertion is zero, so $Z_v=0$. After permuting column blocks, its matrices are $$R=[C\ 0],\qquad T=\operatorname{diag}(C^*C,0),\qquad
 U=\operatorname{diag}(C^*N_vC,0),\qquad S=CC^*.$$ The Hessian cost is unchanged by zero extension, so its row and column costs are exactly $V^e_{v,w}$ and $Y^e_{v,w}$. Equation <a href="#eq:heat-plane-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-plane-identities">[eq:heat-plane-identities]</a> now proves <a href="#eq:heat-single-edge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-single-edge">[eq:heat-single-edge]</a>. Also $e^e_{v,w}\le\|C\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^3$, so Lemma <a href="#lem:initial-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:initial-energy">13</a> gives $J^e_{v,w}(s_0)\le M n_e^3$. Integrate <a href="#eq:heat-single-edge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-single-edge">[eq:heat-single-edge]</a> and discard the nonnegative terminal energy to obtain <a href="#eq:heat-single-edge-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-single-edge-integral">[eq:heat-single-edge-integral]</a>. Finally $CC^*\le S_v$, while $CN_wC^*$ is supported on $CC^*$. Lemma <a href="#lem:order" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:order">6</a> with factor one gives $Y_{v,w}\le Y^e_{v,w}$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:smooth"></a>
### Repeated masks and smooth annuli

The fixed-array dissipation from Section <a href="#sec:heat" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:heat">4</a> controls smooth annuli as long as we also pay for changes in the dual array. We now allow up to $n$ disjoint intervals at every output point. The useful feature of the jump estimate is its dependence on the dual norm: every mixed jump term contains that norm twice. A rescaling will then turn the linear switch count into a square-root loss.

<a id="eq:smooth-kernels"></a>
Define the odd windows and their scale integrals by $$\label{eq:smooth-kernels}
 k_s(t)=\frac{t}{\sqrt s}g_s(t),\qquad
 I_{\varepsilon,R}(t)=\int_{\varepsilon^2}^{R^2}
       (k_s*k_s*k_s)(t)\,\frac{\,\mathrm ds}{s}.$$

<a id="stmt-prop-smooth-switches"></a>
<div id="prop:smooth-switches" class="proposition">

**Proposition 15** (Smooth annuli with repeated choices). *Let $G_v\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^2)$ be complex. At each output pair $(a,b)$, make a step choice of at most $n\ge1$ disjoint radius intervals $(\varepsilon_j,R_j)$ and coefficients $|\beta_j|\le1$. For $$\mathcal K_{a,b}(t)=\sum_j\beta_j I_{\varepsilon_j,R_j}(t)$$ one has $$|\Lambda_{\mathcal K}(G_0,G_1,G_2)|
 \le C\sqrt n\prod_{v=0}^2\|G_v\|_3.$$ The constant is independent of the step partition, the menu of choices, and all endpoints.*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### The cost of the switches

<a id="eq:mask-switch-count"></a>
Work first on the finite grid of Section <a href="#sec:heat" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:heat">4</a>. Make the stated interval and coefficient choices separately for each pair $(i,j)$ and put <a id="eq:repeated-mask"></a>$$\label{eq:repeated-mask}
 A_0^s(i,j)=A_0(i,j)\sum_{\ell}\beta_\ell
                  \mathbf1_{\{\varepsilon_\ell^2<s<R_\ell^2\}}.$$ The sum runs through the list chosen at this particular index pair. Keep $A_1,A_2$ fixed. Choose a compact positive scale interval $[s_-,s_+]$ containing all switch scales in its interior, and take $h^2\le s_-$. By disjointness, $$\label{eq:mask-switch-count}
 |A_0^s(i,j)|\le |A_0(i,j)|,
 \qquad
 \sum_\tau|A_0^{\tau+}(i,j)-A_0^{\tau-}(i,j)|
 \le2n|A_0(i,j)|.$$ The sum is over the distinct switch scales, and one-sided values are used. Both inequalities hold for complex coefficients, including simultaneous openings and closings.

<a id="stmt-lem-repeated-dissipation"></a>
<div id="lem:repeated-dissipation" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:repeated-dissipation"></a>
**Lemma 16** (Repeated-mask dissipation). *Use the masked arrays above and variances $\sigma_v=\alpha_vs$, where $\boldsymbol\alpha$ is any permutation of $(1,11/10,11/10)$. With $n_v$ denoting the norms of the original arrays, $$\label{eq:repeated-dissipation}
 \int_{s_-}^{s_+}\int_{\Pi_0}\sum_vV_v\,\mathrm d\pi_0\,\mathrm ds
 \le C\left(\sum_vn_v^3+n\bigl(n_0^3+n_0^2(n_1+n_2)\bigr)\right).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-repeated-dissipation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-repeated-dissipation</summary>

<a id="eq:switch-entry-bound"></a>
*Proof.* Between switch scales the arrays are fixed, so <a href="#eq:heat-strict" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-strict">[eq:heat-strict]</a> applies. It remains to bound the energy jumps. At a switch $\tau$, freeze the weights and set $\delta A(i,j)=A_0^{\tau+}(i,j)-A_0^{\tau-}(i,j)$. Interpolate all changed entries linearly. Their moduli stay bounded by $|A_0|$ by convexity. For fixed centers and $(i,j)$ let $$\begin{aligned}
 X^2&=\sum_m|A_0(i,m)|^2w_1(m),&
 Y^2&=\sum_m|A_0(m,j)|^2w_0(m),\\
 H^2&=\sum_m|A_2(m,i)|^2w_2(m),&
 Q^2&=\sum_m|A_1(j,m)|^2w_2(m).
\end{aligned}$$ These quantities use the original arrays, and square roots are nonnegative. In $R_0=[W_0\ W_2^*]$, the changing entry has row norm at most $\sqrt{w_0(i)}(X+H)$, column norm at most $\sqrt{w_1(j)}Y$, and derivative modulus $\sqrt{w_0(i)w_1(j)}|\delta A(i,j)|$. The adjoint entry in $R_1=[W_1\ W_0^*]$ instead has row and column bounds $\sqrt{w_1(j)}(Y+Q)$ and $\sqrt{w_0(i)}X$. The star $R_2$ is unchanged. Lemma <a href="#lem:rank-change" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rank-change">9</a> therefore gives $$\label{eq:switch-entry-bound}
 |\Delta e_0|+|\Delta e_1|
 \le C\sum_{i,j}w_0(i)w_1(j)|\delta A(i,j)|
                    (2XY+HY+QX).$$

The measure $h^{-2}w_0(i)w_1(j)\,\mathrm d\pi_0$ is a probability measure. Under it, $p_0,p_1$ are independent normals with means $u_i,u_j$ and variances $\sigma_0,\sigma_1$, while $p_2=-p_0-p_1$. Adding Gaussian variances gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E w_0(m)&=h g_{2\sigma_0}(u_m-u_i),\\
 \mathbb E w_1(m)&=h g_{2\sigma_1}(u_m-u_j),\\
 \mathbb E w_2(m)&=h g_{\sigma_0+\sigma_1+\sigma_2}(u_m+u_i+u_j).
\end{aligned}$$ Extend all arrays by zero to $\mathbb Z^2$. Lemma <a href="#lem:line-maximal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:line-maximal">4</a> bounds $\mathbb E X^2,\mathbb E Y^2,\mathbb E H^2,\mathbb E Q^2$ by constant multiples of, respectively, $$\begin{aligned}
 X_*^2(i,j)&=M_{\mathbb Z}(|A_0(i,\cdot)|^2)(j),&
 Y_*^2(i,j)&=M_{\mathbb Z}(|A_0(\cdot,j)|^2)(i),\\
 H_*^2(i,j)&=M_{\mathbb Z}(|A_2(\cdot,i)|^2)(-i-j),&
 Q_*^2(i,j)&=M_{\mathbb Z}(|A_1(j,\cdot)|^2)(-i-j).
\end{aligned}$$ All these majorants are independent of the switch scale. Cauchy–Schwarz under the probability measure controls the three products in <a href="#eq:switch-entry-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:switch-entry-bound">[eq:switch-entry-bound]</a>. Their square-root majorants have $L^3(\mathbb Z^2,h^2)$ norms at most $Cn_0,Cn_0,Cn_2,Cn_1$. This follows from the $L^{3/2}$ line maximal inequality, applied on slices; the changes $j\mapsto-i-j$ or $i\mapsto-i-j$ are bijections on the relevant slices.

Integrate <a href="#eq:switch-entry-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:switch-entry-bound">[eq:switch-entry-bound]</a>, sum the jumps using <a href="#eq:mask-switch-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mask-switch-count">[eq:mask-switch-count]</a>, and apply Hölder on the grid. The result is $$\sum_\tau\left|\Delta\sum_vJ_v(0,\tau)\right|
 \le Cn\bigl(n_0^3+n_0^2(n_1+n_2)\bigr).$$ Finally integrate <a href="#eq:heat-strict" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-strict">[eq:heat-strict]</a> between consecutive switches. The resulting sum is bounded by the initial energy plus the sum of absolute jumps, after discarding the nonnegative terminal energy. Lemma <a href="#lem:initial-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:initial-energy">13</a> bounds the initial energy by $C\sum_vn_v^3$. This proves <a href="#eq:repeated-dissipation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:repeated-dissipation">[eq:repeated-dissipation]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### From the energy to the smooth kernel

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-repeated-dissipation-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-repeated-dissipation</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:smooth-switches" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:smooth-switches">15</a>.* Put $\lambda=11/10$ and use base variances $\lambda s$ at all vertices. In Lemma <a href="#lem:mixed-trace" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:mixed-trace">10</a>, insert the diagonal matrix with entries $k_s(u_i-p_v)/g_{\lambda s}(u_i-p_v)$ at vertex $v$. It is Hermitian and uniformly bounded: the polynomial factor in $k_s$ is absorbed by the extra Gaussian decay relative to $g_{\lambda s}$.

For the cost at vertex $v$, narrow just its row variance to $s$. Write $T_v^{\rm b}$ for the base Gram and $T_v^{\rm n}$ for the narrowed-row Gram. Directly from the densities, $$(R_v^{\rm b})^*D_vR_v^{\rm b}=\sqrt s\,U_v^{\rm n},
 \qquad T_v^{\rm n}\le\sqrt\lambda\,T_v^{\rm b}.$$ Invertible row scaling preserves the support. Lemma <a href="#lem:order" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:order">6</a> therefore bounds the base cost by $C sV_v^{\rm n}$. The narrowed variances are one of the permutations allowed in Lemma <a href="#lem:repeated-dissipation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:repeated-dissipation">16</a>.

Integrate the mixed trace inequality over base centers in $\Pi_0$ and over $s$ with measure $\,\mathrm ds/s$. Its right side is bounded by <a href="#eq:repeated-dissipation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:repeated-dissipation">[eq:repeated-dissipation]</a>. On the left, the density at each vertex is now $h k_s(u_i-p_v)$. Integration over the center plane convolves the three windows, giving $$h^3\sum_{i,j,l} A_0(i,j)A_1(j,l)A_2(l,i)
       \sum_{\text{chosen at }(i,j)}\beta\,
       I_{\varepsilon,R}(u_i+u_j+u_l).$$ All signed integrals here are absolutely convergent for fixed arrays on the compact positive scale interval.

If the original norms are nonzero, apply this bound to arrays normalized to have norms $n^{-1/2},1,1$. Its right side is bounded by an absolute constant, since $n(n^{-3/2}+2n^{-1})$ is bounded. Rescaling gives $C\sqrt n\prod_vn_v$. The zero-norm case is immediate. The Riemann passage described in Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a> now proves the proposition. The finitely many endpoints are fixed before the mesh tends to zero, so its scale restriction causes no loss. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### The hard endpoint errors

The smooth annuli differ from the hard Hilbert annuli by a difference of two localized odd kernels. We record the exact identity because the next two sections must estimate their sum, rather than pay a constant separately for each of the up to $2n$ endpoints.

<a id="eq:hard-smooth"></a>
Put $$\psi=k_1*k_1*k_1=-g_3''',\qquad
 P(v)=2\int_0^v\psi(z)\,\mathrm dz\quad(v\ge0),\qquad
 c_0=P(\infty)=2g_3''(0)\ne0.$$ The function $P$ has a smooth even extension and vanishes to second order at zero. For $\rho>0$ define <a id="eq:endpoint-kernel"></a>$$\label{eq:endpoint-kernel}
 E_\rho(t)=\frac{P(|t|/\rho)-c_0\mathbf1_{\{|t|>\rho\}}}{t},$$ with the continuous value zero at $t=0$. Gaussian scaling and the substitution $z=|t|/\sqrt s$ in <a href="#eq:smooth-kernels" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:smooth-kernels">[eq:smooth-kernels]</a> yield, away from endpoint values, $$\label{eq:hard-smooth}
 I_{\varepsilon,R}(t)
 =\frac{P(|t|/\varepsilon)-P(|t|/R)}{t}
 =\frac{c_0}{t}\mathbf1_{\{\varepsilon<|t|<R\}}
       +E_\varepsilon(t)-E_R(t).$$ Here $k_1=-g_1'$, so convolution gives $\psi=-g_3'''$; integrating this derivative gives the stated value of $c_0$. Each $E_\rho$ is an odd dilate of a kernel with Gaussian decay and two jumps. The next section proves a frequency-block estimate that can accommodate many such jumps without a linear loss in their number.

<a id="sec:frequency"></a>
### A uniform frequency-block estimate

The rough kernels arising from hard truncation will be decomposed into frequency blocks. The estimate in this section permits their coefficients to depend on the output point and on frequency. Its decisive hypothesis is a pointwise square-sum bound over scales and frequencies; no sum of frequency suprema is required.

Fix $q>0$, let $D\geq1$, and let $\Xi_D$ be a measurable subset of $[-2D,2D]$, equipped with the measure $\,\mathrm d\gamma(\xi)=\,\mathrm d\xi/D$. Thus $\gamma(\Xi_D)\leq4$. Define <a id="eq:frequency-windows"></a>$$\label{eq:frequency-windows}
 \ell_\xi(z)=D^{-1}\frac{\,\mathrm d}{\,\mathrm dz}
                 \bigl(g_q(z)e^{i\xi z}\bigr),
 \qquad H_\xi=\ell_\xi*\ell_\xi*\ell_\xi.$$ The parameters $q,D$ are suppressed in this notation. Constants below may depend on $q$, but not on $D$.

<a id="stmt-prop-frequency-block"></a>
<div id="prop:frequency-block" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:frequency-coefficient-condition"></a>
<a id="eq:frequency-mesh"></a>
<a id="eq:frequency-finite-estimate"></a>
**Proposition 17** (Frequency-block estimate). *Let $\mathcal I\subset\mathbb Z$ be finite and nonempty, and set $L_k=2^k$. Let $A_0,A_1,A_2$ be finite arrays on the lattice $u_i=ih$, with the norms $n_v$ defined in the heat-flow preliminaries. Suppose that measurable coefficients $\mu_{ij,k}:\Xi_D\to\mathbb C$ satisfy $$\label{eq:frequency-coefficient-condition}
 |\mu_{ij,k}(\xi)|\leq C_1,
 \qquad
 \sum_{k\in\mathcal I}\int_{\Xi_D}
           |\mu_{ij,k}(\xi)|^2\,\,\mathrm d\gamma(\xi)\leq C_1^2$$ for every output index pair $(i,j)$, with the first inequality holding almost everywhere. If $$\label{eq:frequency-mesh}
 h^2\leq\frac{q\min_{k\in\mathcal I}L_k^2}{16D^2},$$ then $$\label{eq:frequency-finite-estimate}
 \left|h^3\int_{\Xi_D}\sum_{k\in\mathcal I}\sum_{i,j,l}
 A_0(i,j)\mu_{ij,k}(\xi)A_1(j,l)A_2(l,i)
 \mathop{\mathrm{Dil}}_{L_k}H_\xi(u_i+u_j+u_l)\,\,\mathrm d\gamma(\xi)\right|
 \leq C(q,C_1)\prod_{v=0}^2n_v.$$*

</div>

There are two reasons for refining one Gaussian variance in the proof. First, a window oscillating at frequency $D$ becomes an average of score insertions at variance comparable to $D^{-2}s$. Second, the boundary energy produced by changing the output array can still be estimated uniformly at this small variance. More precisely, the refined row scores will be controlled by the heat identity through single-edge energies and the changes of star energy at slab boundaries. Such a change contains the square of the changing edge norm times the norm of the unchanged edge. We isolate a uniform estimate for that mixed term before beginning the main proof.

<a id="stmt-lem-frequency-boundary"></a>
<div id="lem:frequency-boundary" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:frequency-boundary-mixed"></a>
<a id="eq:frequency-boundary-norm"></a>
**Lemma 18** (A uniform mixed boundary estimate). *Fix distinct vertices $v,w\in\{0,1\}$, so that $\{v,w\}=\{0,1\}$. Let the edge joining $v,w$ have array $B$, indexed in the original endpoint order $(0,1)$, and let the edge joining $v,2$ have array $E$, indexed in the order $(v,2)$. Use variances $(\sigma_v,\sigma_w,\sigma_2)=(\theta s,s,s)$, where $0<\theta\leq1/16$ and $h^2\leq\theta s$. Write $\,\mathrm d\nu_s(d)=g_{(1-\theta)s}(d)\,\,\mathrm dd$. There is a nonnegative array $\mathcal H_E(i,j)$, independent of $s,\theta$, such that $$\begin{aligned}
 &\int_{\mathbb R}\int_{\Pi_d}
       \|C_{v,w}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\|C_{v,2}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}
                  \,\,\mathrm d\pi_d\,\,\mathrm d\nu_s(d)
 \leq C\sum_{i,j}h^2|B(i,j)|^2\mathcal H_E(i,j),
 \label{eq:frequency-boundary-mixed}\\
 &\left(\sum_{i,j}h^2\mathcal H_E(i,j)^3\right)^{1/3}
 \leq C\left(\sum_{m,l}h^2|E(m,l)|^3\right)^{1/3}.
 \label{eq:frequency-boundary-norm}
\end{aligned}$$ Here $(i,j)$ are always in the original order of the endpoints of edge $0$; $i_v,i_w$ denote the same two indices in the order $(v,w)$. The input arrays $B,E$ are extended by zero to $\mathbb Z^2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-frequency-boundary"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-frequency-boundary</summary>

*Proof.* Expand the square of the first Hilbert–Schmidt norm. For each pair $(i,j)$, the measure $$h^{-2}w_v(i_v)w_w(i_w)\,\,\mathrm d\pi_d\,\,\mathrm d\nu_s(d)$$ is a probability measure. Indeed, with $p_v,p_w$ as the free coordinates on $\Pi_d$, it makes $p_v,p_w,d$ independent normal variables with respective means $u_{i_v},u_{i_w},0$ and variances $\theta s,s,(1-\theta)s$. The remaining center is $p_2=d-p_v-p_w$.

<a id="eq:frequency-covariance-domination"></a>
Under this probability measure, the expectation of $h^{-2}w_v(m)w_2(l)$ is a bivariate Gaussian density, evaluated at $(u_m,u_l)$. Its mean is $(u_{i_v},-u_{i_v}-u_{i_w})$, and its covariance matrix is $$s\begin{pmatrix}2\theta&-\theta\\-\theta&3\end{pmatrix}.$$ Call this covariance matrix $\Sigma$. The bounds $\Sigma\leq4s\operatorname{diag}(\theta,1)$ and $\det\Sigma=\theta(6-\theta)s^2$ show, directly from the Gaussian density formula, that $$\label{eq:frequency-covariance-domination}
 \mathbb E[w_v(m)w_2(l)]
 \leq Ch^2g_{C'\theta s}(u_m-u_{i_v})
                g_{C's}(u_l+u_{i_v}+u_{i_w}),$$ where $C$ is absolute and one may take $C'=4$.

Let $M_{\mathbb Z}^{(1)}$ and $M_{\mathbb Z}^{(2)}$ be the discrete line maximal operators in the two coordinates. By Lemma <a href="#lem:line-maximal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:line-maximal">4</a>, we can take <a id="eq:frequency-boundary-majorant"></a>$$\label{eq:frequency-boundary-majorant}
 \mathcal H_E(i,j)^2
 =C\bigl(M_{\mathbb Z}^{(1)}M_{\mathbb Z}^{(2)}|E|^2\bigr)
                 (i_v,-i_v-i_w).$$ In fact, <a href="#eq:frequency-covariance-domination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frequency-covariance-domination">[eq:frequency-covariance-domination]</a> and $h^2\leq\theta s$ show that $\mathbb E\|C_{v,2}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\leq\mathcal H_E(i,j)^2$. Cauchy–Schwarz under this probability measure now proves <a href="#eq:frequency-boundary-mixed" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frequency-boundary-mixed">[eq:frequency-boundary-mixed]</a>. The map $(i,j)\mapsto(i_v,-i_v-i_w)$ is a bijection of $\mathbb Z^2$. The $\ell^{3/2}$ bounds for the two line maximal operators, applied to $|E|^2$, prove <a href="#eq:frequency-boundary-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frequency-boundary-norm">[eq:frequency-boundary-norm]</a>. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-frequency-boundary-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-frequency-boundary</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:frequency-block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:frequency-block">17</a>.* We may discard a common null set of frequencies so that all pointwise bounds in <a href="#eq:frequency-coefficient-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frequency-coefficient-condition">[eq:frequency-coefficient-condition]</a> hold. Set <a id="eq:frequency-slabs"></a>$$\label{eq:frequency-slabs}
 B_{0,k}^\xi(i,j)=A_0(i,j)\mu_{ij,k}(\xi),\qquad
 a_k=qL_k^2,\qquad
 \theta=\frac1{16D^2}.$$ For each $\xi$, use $B_{0,k}^\xi$ on the open slab $2a_k<s<3a_k$, and use the zero array on edge $0$ outside these slabs. The slabs are pairwise disjoint because $a_{k+1}=4a_k$. The other two arrays remain $A_1,A_2$ throughout. All scale integrals below are over $$[s_-,s_+]=[\min_{k\in\mathcal I}a_k,
                         4\max_{k\in\mathcal I}a_k].$$ The mesh hypothesis gives $h^2\leq\theta s$ on this entire interval.

We first turn the oscillatory windows into refined row scores. We then bound the integrated row scores by telescoping the star energies, using Lemma <a href="#lem:frequency-boundary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:frequency-boundary">18</a> at every slab boundary.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
###### Refining a row variance.

Fix a slab, put $L=L_k$, $a=a_k$, and $\omega=\xi/L$. The window at each vertex is <a id="eq:frequency-scaled-window"></a>$$\label{eq:frequency-scaled-window}
 f_{\xi,k}(u):=\mathop{\mathrm{Dil}}_L\ell_\xi(u)
   =\frac LD\frac{\,\mathrm d}{\,\mathrm du}
                  \bigl(g_a(u)e^{i\omega u}\bigr).$$ Initially give all three vertices the variance $s$. The diagonal insertion with entries $f_{\xi,k}(u_i-p_v)/g_s(u_i-p_v)$ has operator norm at most $C(q)$, uniformly on $2a<s<3a$. Indeed, $|L\omega/D|\leq2$, and the remaining factor is bounded using Gaussian decay in $g_a/g_s$. Split this diagonal into its real and imaginary parts before applying Lemma <a href="#lem:mixed-trace" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:mixed-trace">10</a>. The resulting eight terms involve only Hermitian insertions of uniformly bounded norm.

<a id="eq:frequency-gram-average"></a>
Consider the cost belonging to the row star $R_v$. Replace its row variance $s$ by $b'=\theta s$, leave both column variances equal to $s$, and shift the row center from $p_v$ to $p_v+d$. Denote the refined Gram and score insertion by $T_v^d$ and $U_v^d$. Addition of Gaussian variances gives $$\label{eq:frequency-gram-average}
 T_v=\int_{\mathbb R}T_v^d\,\,\mathrm d\nu_s(d),
 \qquad \,\mathrm d\nu_s(d)=g_{s-b'}(d)\,\,\mathrm dd.$$ All these Grams have a common support: changing a strictly positive row density amounts to invertible row scaling and leaves the kernel of the row matrix unchanged.

<a id="eq:frequency-modulated-gaussian"></a>
<a id="eq:frequency-score-refinement"></a>
<a id="eq:frequency-anisotropic-variances"></a>
To represent the inserted Gram in the same way, put $\omega'=a\omega/(a-b')$. Since $b'<a/2$, completing the square gives the exact identity $$\label{eq:frequency-modulated-gaussian}
 g_a(u)e^{i\omega u}
 =\exp\!\left(\frac{\omega^2ab'}{2(a-b')}\right)
       \int_{\mathbb R}g_{b'}(u-d)e^{i\omega'd}g_{a-b'}(d)\,\,\mathrm dd.$$ The exponential prefactor is at most $C(q)$, because $\omega^2b'\leq C(q)$. Also $$g_{a-b'}(d)\leq Cg_{s-b'}(d),\qquad
 \frac LD\leq C(q)\sqrt{\theta s}.$$ Differentiate <a href="#eq:frequency-modulated-gaussian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frequency-modulated-gaussian">[eq:frequency-modulated-gaussian]</a> in $u$. The derivative of the first Gaussian supplies the negative refined row score $-(u-d)/b'$. Consequently, for either the real or the imaginary diagonal insertion, its inserted Gram $K_v$ has the form $$K_v=\int_{\mathbb R}\alpha(d)U_v^d\,\,\mathrm d\nu_s(d),
 \qquad \alpha(d)=\operatorname{Re}c(d)
       \quad\hbox{or}\quad\operatorname{Im}c(d),$$ where the scalar coefficient is explicitly $$c(d)=-\frac LD
       \exp\!\left(\frac{\omega^2ab'}{2(a-b')}\right)
       e^{i\omega'd}\frac{g_{a-b'}(d)}{g_{s-b'}(d)}.$$ The preceding bounds give $|\alpha(d)|\leq C(q)\sqrt{\theta s}$. The averaged matrices are integrable, since the finite-array Gaussian weights dominate the polynomial score factors. By <a href="#eq:frequency-gram-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frequency-gram-average">[eq:frequency-gram-average]</a>, Lemma <a href="#lem:joint-convexity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:joint-convexity">8</a>, and the quadratic homogeneity of $b_T$ in its second argument, $$\label{eq:frequency-score-refinement}
 b_{T_v}(K_v)
 \leq C(q)\theta s\int_{\mathbb R}
           V_v(p_0,\ldots,p_v+d,\ldots,p_2;s)\,\,\mathrm d\nu_s(d).$$ The cost on the right uses variances $$\label{eq:frequency-anisotropic-variances}
 \sigma_v=\theta s,\qquad \sigma_w=s\quad(w\ne v).$$ Thus the factor $\theta$ in the refined score is gained at exactly the scale needed to accommodate the oscillation.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
###### Integrated refined scores.

Fix $v$ and use <a href="#eq:frequency-anisotropic-variances" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frequency-anisotropic-variances">[eq:frequency-anisotropic-variances]</a> for the rest of this part of the proof. All stars, energies, and costs refer to the slab-dependent edge $0$ just defined. Dependence on $\xi$ is suppressed until frequency integration is needed. For a center-dependent cost $Q$, write $$\widetilde Q(s)=\int_{\mathbb R}\int_{\Pi_d}Q(p,s)
                         \,\,\mathrm d\pi_d\,\,\mathrm d\nu_s(d),\qquad
 \widetilde J_v(s)=\int_{\mathbb R}J_v(d,s)\,\,\mathrm d\nu_s(d).$$ On each open interval where the arrays are fixed, the heat identities <a href="#eq:heat-plane-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-plane-identities">[eq:heat-plane-identities]</a> give <a id="eq:frequency-averaged-heat"></a>$$\label{eq:frequency-averaged-heat}
 2\partial_s\widetilde J_v
   =3\widetilde Z_v-\theta\widetilde V_v
                           -\sum_{w\ne v}\widetilde Y_{v,w}.$$ Here the coefficient $3$ has a useful interpretation. The original plane second derivative has coefficient $2+\theta$. Differentiating $\nu_s$, applying its heat equation, and integrating twice by parts adds $1-\theta$. The second heat identity identifies the averaged plane second derivative with $\widetilde Z_v$.

For fixed arrays on a compact positive scale interval, Lemma <a href="#lem:heat-identities" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:heat-identities">11</a> bounds the plane integrals of the energies, costs, and relevant absolute derivatives by polynomials in $|d|$. The density $\nu_s$ and its derivatives have Gaussian decay. These bounds justify differentiation under the integral and the two integrations by parts in $d$, and give the required one-sided limits at slab boundaries. Their constants may depend on the mesh and $\theta$; they justify the identities and do not enter the estimates.

<a id="eq:frequency-score-differential"></a>
<a id="eq:frequency-edge-dissipation"></a>
The metric Cauchy–Schwarz inequality gives $2Z_v\leq\sum_{w\ne v}Y_{v,w}$. Hence $$\label{eq:frequency-score-differential}
 \theta\widetilde V_v
 \leq-2\partial_s\widetilde J_v
                         +\frac12\sum_{w\ne v}\widetilde Y_{v,w}.$$ The column costs in this inequality can be paid for by individual edges. Indeed, $S_v\geq C_{v,w}C_{v,w}^*$, so the order comparison of Lemma <a href="#lem:order" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:order">6</a> yields <a id="eq:frequency-edge-cost"></a>$$\label{eq:frequency-edge-cost}
 Y_{v,w}\leq
 b_{C_{v,w}C_{v,w}^*}(C_{v,w}N_wC_{v,w}^*)=:Y^e_{v,w}.$$ The inserted matrix is supported on the single-edge Gram, so this comparison also applies if the larger star Gram has a larger support. Define the integrated single-edge energy $$J^e_{v,w}(s)=\iint_{\mathbb R^2}
                  \Phi(C_{v,w}C_{v,w}^*)\,\,\mathrm dp_v\,\,\mathrm dp_w.$$ The integral of $Y^e_{v,w}$ over $\Pi_d$ is independent of $d$, because only the two endpoint centers occur. With just this edge present, the heat identities give $$\label{eq:frequency-edge-dissipation}
 -2\partial_sJ^e_{v,w}(s)
 \geq\iint_{\mathbb R^2}Y^e_{v,w}(p,s)\,\,\mathrm dp_v\,\,\mathrm dp_w.$$ The coefficient of this column cost is $1$; the remaining row cost is nonnegative. Thus the estimate is independent of the small variance ratio $\theta$.

<a id="eq:frequency-cubic-summation"></a>
If the edge is unchanged throughout $[s_-,s_+]$, integration of <a href="#eq:frequency-edge-dissipation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frequency-edge-dissipation">[eq:frequency-edge-dissipation]</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:single-edge-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:single-edge-energy">14</a> bound its total cost by $C(n_1^3+n_2^3)$. If it is edge $0$, apply the same argument separately on its slabs. The result is bounded by $$C\sum_{k\in\mathcal I}\sum_{i,j}h^2
                         |B_{0,k}^\xi(i,j)|^3.$$ After integration in $\xi$, this is at most $C(C_1)n_0^3$, since $$\label{eq:frequency-cubic-summation}
 \sum_k\int_{\Xi_D}|B_{0,k}^\xi(i,j)|^3\,\,\mathrm d\gamma
 \leq C_1|A_0(i,j)|^3
       \sum_k\int_{\Xi_D}|\mu_{ij,k}(\xi)|^2\,\,\mathrm d\gamma
 \leq C_1^3|A_0(i,j)|^3.$$ We have therefore controlled all column terms in <a href="#eq:frequency-score-differential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frequency-score-differential">[eq:frequency-score-differential]</a>. The remaining issue is the boundary energy of the stars.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
###### Summing the slab boundaries.

<a id="eq:frequency-pointwise-jump"></a>
Before the first slab, edge $0$ is zero. By Lemma <a href="#lem:initial-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:initial-energy">13</a>, $\widetilde J_v(s_-)\leq C(n_1^3+n_2^3)$. The star at vertex $2$ has no jumps. For $v\in\{0,1\}$, let $w$ be the other vertex of edge $0$. At either boundary of a slab, write $C_{\rm sw}=C_{v,w}$ for the slab-side weighted block and $C_{\rm fix}=C_{v,2}$ for the unchanged weighted block. The absolute change in the pointwise star energy is at most $$\label{eq:frequency-pointwise-jump}
 C\bigl(\|C_{\rm sw}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^3
       +\|C_{\rm sw}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\|C_{\rm fix}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\bigr).$$ To verify this bound, integrate the derivative of $\Phi(C_{\rm fix}C_{\rm fix}^*+tC_{\rm sw}C_{\rm sw}^*)$ for $0<t\leq1$. Its support is fixed there, and the derivative is $$\begin{aligned}
 &\frac32\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl((C_{\rm fix}C_{\rm fix}^*
                   +tC_{\rm sw}C_{\rm sw}^*)^{1/2}
                    C_{\rm sw}C_{\rm sw}^*\bigr)\\
 &\qquad\leq\frac32\bigl(\|C_{\rm fix}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}
                    +\|C_{\rm sw}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\bigr)
                      \|C_{\rm sw}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2.
 \end{aligned}$$ Continuity gives the endpoint at $t=0$. Removing the edge has the same absolute change as adding it.

The integrated cubic term in <a href="#eq:frequency-pointwise-jump" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frequency-pointwise-jump">[eq:frequency-pointwise-jump]</a> is bounded by the cube of the norm of $B_{0,k}^\xi$, by Lemma <a href="#lem:initial-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:initial-energy">13</a>. Summing both boundaries of every slab and using <a href="#eq:frequency-cubic-summation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frequency-cubic-summation">[eq:frequency-cubic-summation]</a> gives $C(C_1)n_0^3$ after frequency integration.

For the mixed term, let $A^{(v,2)}$ be the fixed unweighted array on the edge joining $v,2$, indexed in that order. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:frequency-boundary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:frequency-boundary">18</a> with this array and denote its majorant by $\mathcal H_v$. It is independent of $k,\xi,s$, and its cube norm is at most $C(n_1+n_2)$. Both boundaries together consequently contribute at most a constant times $$\begin{aligned}
